[Iran](14653.md) had a [developing](78449.md) [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) that was [centrally planned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy) through its large [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector).[^cite_note-Economist,_Jan_18,_2003-26][^cite_note-27] With [a population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Iran) of over 90 million, the Iranian economy was supported by an industrial manufacturing base, a subsidized oil and gas sector, and abundant natural resources. Its energy sector was home to 10% of the [world's proven oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proven_oil_reserves) and 15% of the [world's gas reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_reserves),[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-EIA-29] often projecting Iran as an "[energy superpower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_superpower)".[^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31]

Since the [1979 Islamic revolution](347268.md), the Iranian economy had experienced slower economic growth, high inflation, and recurring crises. A [variety of armed conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_involving_Iran) in the region have exacerbated economic pressures from the 1980s to the 2020s. The nation had experienced varied levels of "[brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran's_brain_drain)" due to [Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_peoples) seeking overseas employment or immigration.[^cite_note-CIA-32][^cite_note-BBC_drain-33] Inflation in Iran has been persistent and caused mass protests.&#x20;

The [nation's nuclear program](721807.md) and [foreign policy](64709.md) have placed Iran under [sustained international sanctions](25699056.md), deterring [foreign and domestic investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Direct_Investment_in_Iran) with adverse humanitarian impact.[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35] The periodic lifting of these sanctions have supported an emerging tourism industry. Its official currency, the [Iranian rial](708656.md), has fallen significantly since the 2010s, leading to [mass protests](81976180.md) over its purchasing power.[^cite_note-6_charts_bbc-36] Iran maintains low international rankings on [economic freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_freedom)[^cite_note-Heritage-2020-37][^cite_note-Nordea-2020-38] and "[ease of doing business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_doing_business)".[^cite_note-EoDB-39]

## History until 1979

Main article: [Economic history of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Iran)

Further information: [Sasanian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_economy), [Trade in Safavid Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_in_Safavid_Iran), and [History of Iran](64213.md)



Persian [Achaemenid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid) [gold coin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_coin), circa 490 BC

In 546 BC, [Croesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croesus) of [Lydia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydia) was defeated and captured by the [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persia), who then adopted gold as the main metal for their coins.[^cite_note-history-40][^cite_note-41] There are accounts in the biblical [Book of Esther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Esther) of dispatches being sent from Susa to provinces as far out as India and the [Kingdom of Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kush) during the reign of [Xerxes the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerxes_I_of_Persia) (485–465 BC). By the time of [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus) (c. 475 BC), the [Royal Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Road) of the [Persian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Empire) ran some 2,857 km from the city of [Susa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susa) on the Karun (250 km east of the Tigris) to the port of Smyrna (modern [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir) in Turkey) on the [Aegean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea).

Modern [agriculture in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Iran) dates back to the 1850s when [Amir Kabir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amir_Kabir) undertook a number of changes to the traditional agricultural system. Such changes included importing modified seeds and signing collaboration contracts with other countries. [Polyakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lazar_Polyakov)'s *Bank Esteqrazi* was bought in 1898 by the [Tsarist government of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Russia), and later passed into the hands of the Iranian government by a contract in 1920.[^cite_note-Reuter-42] The bank continued its activities under the name of *Bank Iran* until 1933 when incorporating the newly founded [Keshavarzi Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keshavarzi_Bank).[^cite_note-Reuter-42][^cite_note-LOC5-43]

The [Imperial Bank of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Bank_of_Persia) was established in 1885, with offices in all major cities of Persia.[^cite_note-Reuter-42] [Reza Shah Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah_Pahlavi) (r. 1925–41) improved the country's overall infrastructure, implemented educational reform, campaigned against foreign influence, reformed the legal system, and introduced modern industries. During this time, Iran experienced a period of social change, economic development, and relative political stability.[^cite_note-LOC5-43]

Reza Shah Pahlavi, who abdicated in 1941, was succeeded by his son, [Mohammad Reza Shah Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Reza_Shah_Pahlavi) (r. 1941–79). No fundamental change occurred in the [economy of Iran during World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Iran#Pahlavi_Era_\(1925%E2%80%9379\)) and the years immediately following. Between 1954 and 1960 a rapid increase in oil revenues and sustained foreign aid led to greater investment and fast-paced economic growth, primarily in the government sector. Subsequently, inflation increased, the value of the national currency, the rial, depreciated, and a foreign-trade deficit developed. Economic policies implemented to combat these problems led to declines in the rates of nominal economic growth and per capita income by 1961.[^cite_note-LOC5-43]

## History since the 1979 Islamic revolution

|  | This section **contains an excessive amount of intricate [detail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:DETAIL)**. Please help [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Economy_of_Iran\&action=edit) by removing excessive detail that goes against [Wikipedia's inclusion policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:What_Wikipedia_is_not). *(April 2026)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Prior to 1979, Iran developed rapidly, as surging global oil revenues in the 1970s flooded the country with capital, with rapid GDP and per-capita income expansion. Traditionally agricultural, by the 1970s, the country had undergone significant industrialization and modernization.[^cite_note-documentary-44][^cite_note-documentary2-45] The pace slowed by 1978 as [capital flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_of_capital) reached \$30 to \$40 billion 1980-US dollars just before the [revolution](347268.md).[^cite_note-Migration-46] Furthermore, economic prosperity heavily favored urban elites, leaving many rural and traditional communities marginalized. The Shah's earlier land reforms upended traditional rural economies and forced massive, unplanned migration to the cities, forcing migrants into settlements without water or electricity. These deeply religious rural migrants faced extreme poverty while living right next to the wealthy, Westernized elite. Known as the Mostazafin (the oppressed), the marginalized population became the muscle of the street protests against the monarchy, mobilized by the Islamic clergy's promises of social justice.[^cite_note-PBS-47]

Following the revolution, subsequent nationalizations of industries in 1979 and the outbreak of the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War), over 80% of the economy came under government control. The eight-year war with Iraq claimed at least 300,000 Iranian lives and injured more than 500,000. The cost of the war to Iran's economy was some \$500 billion.[^cite_note-48]

After hostilities ceased in 1988, the government tried to develop the country's communications, transportation, manufacturing, health care, education and energy sectors, including its prospective [nuclear power facilities](721807.md), and began integrating its communication and transportation systems with those of neighboring states.[^cite_note-49]

The government's long-term objectives since the revolution were stated as [economic independence](62450.md), [full employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_employment), and a comfortable standard of living but [Iran's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Iran) had more than doubled between 1980 and 2000 and its median age declined.[^cite_note-Dr._Gheissari-50] Although many Iranians are farmers, agricultural production has consistently fallen since the 1960s. By the late 1990s, Iran imported much of its food. At that time, economic hardship in the countryside resulted in many [people moving to cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization).[^cite_note-Migration-46]

Following the war, the reformist [Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar_Hashemi_Rafsanjani)'s administration advocated a fundamental reorientation and liberalization of Iran's economy, along with efforts to reverse Iran's international isolation. Rafsanjani's agenda included infrastructure development, the privatization of state enterprises, foreign exchange liberalization, the establishment of free-trade zones and the elimination of subsidies and price controls. However, this upward trajectory was derailed by domestic political friction and economic policy errors. Uncontrolled government spending and heightened consumer demand triggered severe inflation, sparking riots across multiple Iranian cities in 1992 and 1994. Fearing instability, domestic private entities resisted investing, while international partners were driven away by political volatility and tightened U.S. sanctions implemented after 1995. Simultaneously, weak global oil prices in the early 1990s sparked a major currency and debt crisis. In response, Rafsanjani froze infrastructure projects, reinstated foreign exchange controls, rescheduled foreign debts and abandoned plans to reform state subsidies. Politically, Rafsanjani faced heavy domestic resistance as Islamic leftists committed to state-controlled economics initially blocked his free-market initiatives while traditional conservatives sabotaged his second term by fighting his attempts to loosen Islamic social and cultural laws.[^cite_note-PBS-47]

The 1997 election of [Mohammad Khatami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Khatami) sparked a reform movement by Islamic leftists that ultimately failed to restructure the nation's power dynamics. From its inception, the movement prioritized social, cultural, and political changes with their approach heavily shaped by their deep-seated distrust of the free market and frustration with the corruption and repression of the Rafsanjani administration. Furthermore, fears arose among leftists on the chaotic nature of economic deregulation that could erode the public support for reforms.[^cite_note-PBS-47]

During his presidency, Khatami achieved a solid ground in implementing serious economic restructuring. Among his accomplishments was unifying the official exchange rate with the [parallel exchange rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_exchange_rate), establishing an Oil Stabilization Fund as a cushion against market volatility, authorizing the first post-revolutionary private banks, pushing through some improvements to foreign investment frameworks, stewarding the economy through a tumultuous period of unprecedented low oil revenues and luring new interest and investment from the Western world.[^cite_note-PBS-47]

However, more ambitious plans, including efforts to reduce the costly energy subsidies was met by opposition from conservative parliamentarians as they adopted obstructionist approach to Khatami's economic agenda, as means of subverting his political and cultural reforms. Khatami also found himself confronted with the onset of a global recession in the early 2000s, a deep slump in oil prices, persistent inflation, unemployment and economic mismanagement while his response was a typically cautious array of small-scale economic initiatives that bore little results.[^cite_note-PBS-47]

The election of [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](7907317.md) brought about economic promises with populist appeal, having pledged to the re-distribution of oil revenues during the [2000s commodities boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_commodities_boom), which sustained illusory growth rates and brought an epic influx of revenues and foreign exchange. The rise of Asia as a commercial counterweight to Iran's historic trade partners in Europe, enabled Tehran to blunt the impact of sanctions. After the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md), China became the largest source of Iran's imports, and Asian states purchased more Iranian oil than any other region. Ahmadinejad took an assertive and problematic role on policy by expanding credit and spending in a freewheeling fashion, feuding openly with Central Bank chiefs, dismantling the bureaucracy, disempowering government technocrats and reveling in the reverberations of the global economic meltdown in 2008. His provocative rhetoric on Israel and Iran's continuing defiance of U.N. Security Council resolutions on its nuclear program heightened the sense of political risk and persuaded foreign investors to leave voluntarily.[^cite_note-PBS-47]

Ahmadinejad also move boldly against the longstanding distortions plaguing Iran's economy, such as subsidies and state dominance, but in a counterproductive way. Privatization benefited mainly state-affiliated companies, particularly those associated with the Islamic Revolutionary Guards, whose retirement funds took a majority stake in the state telecommunications firm in 2009. Ahmadinejad's assiduous use of economic issues made him especially vulnerable, especially after mood inside Iran soured as the global economic slowdown in 2009 began to impact Iran and the price of oil crashed to less than one-third of its 2008 high. Senior political figures and renowned economists were also sharply critical of Ahmadinejad's spending and interventionist approach, while strikes by bazaaris in 2008 and 2010 forced the government to delay or abandon planned tax hikes.[^cite_note-PBS-47]

In 2007 the [Iranian subsidy reform plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_subsidy_reform_plan) introduced price controls and subsidies particularly on food and energy.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-Subsidy-52] [Contraband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smuggling_in_Iran), administrative controls, [widespread corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Iran),[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] and other restrictive factors undermine [private sector-led growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatization_in_Iran).[^cite_note-CIA-32] The government's 20-year vision involved market-based reforms reflected in a five-year development plan, 2016 to 2021, focusing on "a resilient economy" and "[progress in science and technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_in_Iran)".[^cite_note-IRI-WB-2020-55] Most of Iran's exports are oil and gas, accounting for a majority of [government revenue](50913538.md#Revenues) in 2010.[^cite_note-56] In March 2022, the Iranian parliament under the then new president [Ebrahim Raisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebrahim_Raisi) decided to eliminate a major subsidy for importing food, medicines and animal feed, valued at \$15 billion in 2021.[^cite_note-ag2022-57] Also in March 2022, 20 billion tons of basic goods exports [from Russia](3912038.md) including vegetable oil, wheat, barley and corn were agreed.[^cite_note-ag2022-57]

Due to its relative isolation from global financial markets, Iran was initially able to avoid recession in the aftermath of the [2008 global financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%932010).[^cite_note-58] However, following [expansion of international sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Iran) related to [Iran's nuclear program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran's_nuclear_program), the [Iranian rial](708656.md) fell to a record low of 23,900 to the US dollar in September 2012.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-businessweek.com-60][^cite_note-WSJ2-61][^cite_note-62]

2025 was marked by a collapsing currency, surging inflation causing widespread unrest. The Iranian rial plummeted to record lows, trading well above one million rials to the U.S. dollar, contributing to inflation rates exceeding 40% and sharply rising prices for basic commodities, causing hardships and nationwide protests.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-\:18-64] By December 2025, the rial weakened to roughly 1.3–1.5 million per U.S. dollar on open markets, imposing heavy pressure on domestic prices and living standards.[^cite_note-\:202-65][^cite_note-66] Inflation surged with annual inflation in 2025 reaching or exceeding the 40% range, with food and essential goods prices in some cases climbing by 50% or more.[^cite_note-\:182-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]

In late December 2025, the government replaced the head of the Central Bank of Iran in an attempt to steady the economy.[^cite_note-70] By early 2026, the crisis had contributed to significant social strain.[^cite_note-71] Official data for January 2026 indicated monthly inflation around 8% overall and nearly 14% for food and beverages. Food inflation alone approached roughly 90% year-on-year. Annual inflation also rose further to about 44.6% in January 2026.[^cite_note-72]

The [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War) with Israel in June 2025 had a [profound short-term impact on Iran's economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_Twelve-Day_War).[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] The economic toll was significant: Iran's oil exports plunged by 94% during the war, dropping from about 1.7 million barrels per day to barely 100,000, due to damage and fear of tanker transit in the Persian Gulf.[^cite_note-\:7-75] On the other side, Israeli and U.S. strikes caused billions in physical damage: dozens of military facilities, communication centers, and at least 120 residential buildings in Tehran were destroyed or severely damaged.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77] After 12 days, Iran agreed to a ceasefire; an Iranian MP on parliament's economic commission admitted Iran's economy "lacks the resilience for a decisive battle" and that the financial strain was a key reason Iran could not prolong the war.[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79] The war's aftermath brought even tighter U.S. sanctions. Enforcement on oil smuggling was intensified by Western navies.[^cite_note-80] The currency, which had plunged during the conflict, remained very weak (around 1.3 million rials per USD by mid-late 2025).[^cite_note-81]

## Macroeconomic trends

See also: [Demographics of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Iran), [Science and technology in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Iran), and [International rankings of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_Iran)



[Nominal price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominal_price) of oil from 1861 to 2020 from [Our World in Data](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_World_in_Data)&#x20;

[Iran's national science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_in_Iran) budget in 2005 was about \$900 million, roughly equivalent to the 1990 figure.[^cite_note-82] By early 2000, Iran allocated around 0.4% of its GDP to [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_in_Iran#Research_expenditure), ranking the country behind the world average of 1.4%.[^cite_note-autogenerated1-83] In 2009 the ratio of research to GDP was 0.87% against the government's medium-term target of 2.5%.[^cite_note-Ministry_of_Commerce-84] Iran ranked first in scientific growth in the world in 2011[^cite_note-85] and 17th in science production in 2012. Iran has a broad and diversified [industrial base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_of_Iran).[^cite_note-86]

According to *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*, Iran ranked 39th in a list of industrialized nations, producing \$23 billion of industrial products in 2008.[^cite_note-87] Between 2008 and 2009 Iran moved to 28th from 69th place in annual industrial production growth because of its relative isolation from the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis)[^cite_note-88] and because the [price of oil](5137675.md) reached an alltime high in the first half of 2008. In the early 21st century the service sector was Iran's largest, followed by industry (mining and manufacturing) and agriculture.

Iran's long-term economic growth since the [1979 Islamic Revolution](347268.md) has been far below its pre-revolution performance. According to [Central Bank](3961876.md) data, GDP grew on average only about 1.9% per year from 1979 to 2020, compared to 9.1% annually in 1960–1979 before the revolution.[^cite_note-\:13-89] The Iran–Iraq War (1980–88) caused a deep [recession](25382.md): during 1981–89 Iran's GDP grew only 0.9% annually on average, the lowest of any period, as war destruction and revolutionary upheaval crippled output.[^cite_note-\:13-89] In the early 1990s, after the war, Iran saw a rebound – [President Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar_Hashemi_Rafsanjani) administration (1989–1997) achieved about 5.5% average growth by rebuilding infrastructure and liberalizing parts of the economy.[^cite_note-\:13-89]

After a modest recovery in 2020–2022, the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) and [World Bank](36752.md) estimated Iran's real GDP growth at around 2–5% in 2022–2024, partly due to higher oil prices and improved non-oil output.[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-\:02-91] 2025 was worse: after new U.S. sanctions and the mid-2025 conflict, the IMF projected near zero growth (0.3%) for 2025, revising down earlier forecasts.[^cite_note-\:22-92] Oil production and exports have been constrained by sanctions and the 2025 war damage; Iran's oil exports, which averaged \~1.4–1.7 million barrels/day in 2024, were expected to drop by several hundred thousand barrels in 2025 under sanctions.[^cite_note-\:22-92][^cite_note-93]

According to the IMF estimations, in 2026 Iran’s economy is roughly one-fifth the size of Saudi Arabia and Turkey, less than half the size of the UAE and Israel, and it is about 20% smaller than Kazakhstan’s economy.[^cite_note-94]

&#x20;The [IMF](15251.md) projects that in 2026 the economy will contract by 6.1% and the inflation rate will reach to 68.9%.[^cite_note-95]

### Reform plan

See also: [Iranian targeted subsidy plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_targeted_subsidy_plan)

Before 2010, expansion of public healthcare and [international relations](64709.md) were the other main objectives of the fifth plan, an ambitious series of measures that included [subsidy reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_targeted_subsidy_plan), banking recapitalization, currency reform, [taxation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Iran), [customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Customs), construction, employment, nationwide goods and services distribution, social justice and productivity.[^cite_note-97] The intent was to make the country self-sufficient by 2015 and replace the payment of \$100 billion in subsidies annually with targeted [social assistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_assistance).[^cite_note-csmonitor1-98][^cite_note-Turq6-99][^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101] These reforms target Iran's major sources of inefficiency and [price distortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distortion_\(economics\)) and are likely to lead to major restructuring of almost all economic sectors.[^cite_note-Turq6-99]

By removing energy subsidies around 2010, Iran intended to make its industries more efficient and competitive.[^cite_note-102] By 2016, one third of Iran's economic growth was expected to originate from productivity improvement. Energy subsidies left the economy as one of the world's least energy-efficient, with [energy intensity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_intensity) three times the global average and 2.5 times higher than the Middle Eastern average.[^cite_note-RFE-103] Notwithstanding its own issues, [the banking sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_Iran) was seen (2010) as a potential hedge against the removal of subsidies, as the plan is not expected to directly impact banks.[^cite_note-104]

## National planning

See also: [Management and Planning Organization of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Management_and_Planning_Organization_of_Iran) and [Environment of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_Iran)

[Iran's budget](64702.md#Budget) is established by the [Management and Planning Organization of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Management_and_Planning_Organization_of_Iran) and proposed by [the government](50913538.md) to the parliament before [the year's end](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_calendar). Following approval of the budget by [Majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis_of_Iran), the [central bank](3961876.md) presents a detailed [monetary and credit policy](3961876.md#Inflation_and_monetary_policy) to the [Money and Credit Council](3961876.md#Money_and_Credit_Council) (MCC) for approval. Thereafter, major elements of these policies are incorporated into the five-year economic development plan.[^cite_note-LOC5-43] The plan is part of "Vision 2025", a strategy for long-term sustainable growth.[^cite_note-105]

## Fiscal policy

Main articles: [Public finance and fiscal policy in Iran](64702.md#Public_finance_and_fiscal_policy), [Taxation in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Iran), and [Inflation and monetary policy in Iran](3961876.md#Inflation_and_monetary_policy)

Since the 1979 revolution, [government spending](50913538.md#Public_finance_and_fiscal_policy) has averaged 59% on social policies, 17% on economic matters, 15% on [national defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_Iran), and 13% on general affairs.[^cite_note-LOC5-43] Payments averaged 39% on [education](1332627.md), [health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Iran) and [social security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_Organization_\(Iran\)), 20% on other social programs, 3% on [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Iran), 16% on [water](18304723.md), [power and gas](26980995.md), 5% on [manufacturing and mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Iran), 12% on [roads and transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Iran) and 5% on other economic affairs.[^cite_note-LOC5-43] Iran's [investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_investment) reached 27.7% of GDP in 2009.[^cite_note-CIA-32] Between 2002 and 2006, inflation fluctuated around 14%.[^cite_note-IndexMundi-134]

In 2008, around 55% of government revenue came from [oil and natural gas revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Petroleum_\(Iran\)#Revenues_from_crude_oil), with 31% from [taxes and fees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Iran).[^cite_note-payvand.com-135][^cite_note-136] There are virtually millions of people who do not pay [taxes in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Iran) and hence operate [outside the formal economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy).[^cite_note-CIA-32] The budget for [year 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_calendar) was \$462 billion, 9% less than 2011.[^cite_note-BusinessWeek-137] The budget is based on an [oil price](5137675.md) of \$85 per barrel. The value of the US dollar is estimated at [IRR](708656.md#Value) 12,260 for the same period.[^cite_note-BusinessWeek-137]

According to the head of the Department of Statistics of Iran, if the [rules of budgeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_process) were observed the government could save at least 30 to 35% on its expenses.[^cite_note-138] The central bank's [interest rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_Iran#Rates) is 21%, and the [inflation rate](3961876.md#Inflation_and_monetary_policy) has climbed to 22% in 2012, 10% higher than in 2011.[^cite_note-139] There is little alignment between [fiscal](50913538.md#Public_finance_and_fiscal_policy) and [monetary policy](3961876.md#Inflation_and_monetary_policy). According to the [Central Bank of Iran](3961876.md), the [gap between the rich and the poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class_in_Iran) narrowed because of [monthly subsidies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_targeted_subsidy_plan#Plan_revision) but the trend could reverse if high inflation persists.[^cite_note-FT4-140]

### Defense-related burden

See also: [Funding of Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funding_of_Hezbollah), [Iranian support for the Houthis](78962952.md), [Ballistic missile program of Iran](79315120.md), and [Nuclear program of Iran](721807.md)



Iran Ministry of Defense New Weapons Ceremony and Young Soldier Festival

According to official data, as of 2023 Iran spends 10.3 billion [USD](18717338.md) or 2.1% of its GDP on national defense. This percentage is similar to that in other countries such as UK, France and [Finland](10577.md).[^cite_note-141] In 2025, the Iranian budget bill granted 51% of the total oil and gas export revenues, estimated at 12 billion euros, to the [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC)](1285472.md) and the [Law Enforcement Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_Enforcement_Command_of_the_Islamic_Republic_of_Iran) (LEF).[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

Iran also finances [Hezbollah](13919.md), the [Yemeni Houthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_movement), Iraqi militia, and [Hamas](13913.md). The average annual budget reserved for the funding of [Iran's proxies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funding_of_the_Axis_of_Resistance) is estimated at US\$1.6 billion.[^cite_note-144] According to Syrian opposition sources, starting from the beginning of 2011, Iran allocated a total of US\$50 billion to maintain the [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) regime in [Syria](7515849.md). However, this investment proved to be a failure following the eventual collapse of the regime in late 2024.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

The most costly of Iran's defense expenditures is its nuclear program. The estimated total cost of Iran's [nuclear program](721807.md) until 2025 approaches US\$500 billion.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] As a result of its nuclear program, Iran is subject to international sanctions, causing a long-term economic stagnation which cost Iran an additional US\$1.2 trillion over 12 years.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] Furthermore, the sanctions led to a significant decline in [foreign direct investments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) (FDI), with Iran experiencing a reduction of approximately 80% in FDI between 2011 and 2021.[^cite_note-151]

## Inflation



Iran inflation

Inflation in Iran has been persistently high since the revolution, eroding living standards. During 1960–1978, Iran's inflation was usually in single digits, but after 1979 it surged into double digits almost every year. From 1979 to 2024, consumer prices increased astronomically – an item that cost 100 rials in 1960 costs over 2 million rials by 2025.[^cite_note-\:3-152] The average inflation rate since 1979 has been about 17–18% per year, compared to under 10% before 1979.[^cite_note-\:3-152][^cite_note-\:02-91] Periodic bouts of very high inflation occurred: in the 1980s during the war and money-printing, inflation topped 20–30%. In the 1990s, it spiked above 50% (in 1995). Tightening measures brought inflation down to \~10% by the early 2000s, but it accelerated again later. International sanctions on Iran's oil and banking (especially 2012 and 2018 onward) led to currency devaluation and shortages, driving inflation to 30–50% annually in 2018–2023.[^cite_note-153] By 2023–2024, Iran's annual inflation was roughly 40%, particularly elevated for food prices, which led to steep declines in real incomes.[^cite_note-154] In 2025, amid renewed sanctions and war disruption, the IMF raised Iran's projected inflation to over 43%, placing Iran as the 4th-highest inflation country globally (after [Venezuela](32374.md), [Sudan](27421.md), [Zimbabwe](34399.md)).[^cite_note-\:22-92][^cite_note-155] The latest data shows that the annual inflation rate reached 38.7% in May 2025.[^cite_note-\:0-156] Due to the high inflation and the devaluation of the Iranian real (90% since 2018)[^cite_note-\:0-156] have brought the Iran's parliament to approve a bill in committee that paves the way to remove four zeros from the national currency, the rial, in an effort to tackle long-term inflation. However, experts say that while cutting zeros from the currency may have some benefits, it does not offer a clear solution to Iran's deeper economic problems.[^cite_note-\:0-156]

In August 2025, Iran effectively raised taxation by introducing a new law making inflation partly taxable framing it as a tool against hoarding in a country where inflation often exceeds 40 percent.[^cite_note-157]

As of April 2026, the IMF forecasts that the inflation in Iran will hit 69%.[^cite_note-158]

## Public finances

Iran had an estimated \$110 billion in [foreign reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_reserves) in 2011[^cite_note-F10-159] and balances its [external payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_payment) by pricing oil at approximately \$75 per barrel.[^cite_note-\:2-160] As of 2013, only \$30 to \$50 billion of those reserves are accessible because of current sanctions.[^cite_note-ABC-161] [Iranian media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_media) has questioned the reason behind Iran's government non-repatriation of [its foreign reserves](3961876.md#Foreign_reserves) before the imposition of the [latest round of sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1929) and its failure to convert into [gold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_bullion). As a consequence, the [Iranian rial](708656.md) lost more than 40% of its value between December 2011 and April 2012.[^cite_note-FT4-140]

Iran's external and fiscal accounts reflect falling oil prices in FY 2012, but remain in surplus. The current account was expected to reach a surplus of 2.1% of GDP in FY 2012, and the net fiscal balance (after payments to Iran's [National Development Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Development_Fund)) will register a surplus of 0.3% of GDP.[^cite_note-EIU5-162] In 2013 the [external debts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/External_debt) stood at \$7.2 billion down from \$17.3 billion in 2012.[^cite_note-\:14-163] Overall [fiscal deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_deficit) is expected to deteriorate to 2.7% of GDP in FY 2016 from 1.7% in 2015.[^cite_note-IMF111-164] [Money in circulation](3961876.md#Money_supply) reached \$700 billion in March 2020 (based on the 2017 [pre-devaluation exchange rate](708656.md#Value)), thus furthering the decline of the [Iranian rial](708656.md) and rise in inflation.[^cite_note-\:15-165][^cite_note-\:16-166]

### Iranian rial

The [Iranian rial](708656.md) has continually lost value, especially since the 2010s. The currency was roughly 70 rials per US dollar before 1979; after the revolution and war, it fell to \~1,700 rials/USD by 1990s, and kept sliding.[^cite_note-fred.stlouisfed.org-167] Following the U.S. withdrawal from the nuclear deal (2018) and sanctions, the rial's decline accelerated: the free-market exchange rate crossed 300,000 rials/USD in 2020, then 600,000 by late 2022, and hit 1,000,000 rials per USD in early 2025 – a psychological milestone of 1 million.[^cite_note-fred.stlouisfed.org-167][^cite_note-168] Authorities periodically attempted currency reforms, e.g., banning exchange shops, unifying rates, or proposing to cut four zeros from the currency, but the underlying pressures of limited foreign exchange and excess money supply have kept the rial weak.[^cite_note-169] By 2025, Iran's currency and inflation problems were severe enough that even basic goods saw rapid price rises, sparking protests over living costs.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] Subsidy reforms led to jumps in prices of staples like bread and medicine.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173]

## Corruption

Main article: [Corruption in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Iran)

This section is an excerpt from [Corruption in Iran § In the Islamic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Iran#In_the_Islamic_Republic).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Corruption_in_Iran\&action=edit)]

Transparency International defines corruption as the "abuse of entrusted power for personal gain".[^cite_note-174] Its annual Corruption Perceptions Index evaluates perceived corruption in the public sector of many countries (182 in 2025) on a "combination of at least 3 data sources drawn from 13 different corruption surveys and assessments. ... collected by a variety of reputable institutions, including the World Bank and the [World Economic Forum](172185.md)".[^cite_note-175] The 2025 Index scored Iran at 23 on a scale from 0 ("highly corrupt") to 100 ("very clean") where 42 was the global average. The country perceived to be least corrupt (Denmark) scored 89, and the two perceived most corrupt (Somalia and South Sudan) scored 9.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_Iran_CPI2025-Iran-176] Regionally, the average score among Middle Eastern and North African countries[^cite_note-178] was 39. The best score in the region was 69 (the United Arab Emirates) and the worst scores were 12 (Libya and Yemen).[^cite_note-179]

Since the current version of the Corruption Perceptions Index was introduced in 2012, Iran's score has steadily declined from its high point of 30 in 2017 to its lowest-ever scores of 23 in 2024 and 2025.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_Iran_CPI2025-Iran-176] Its score in the [World Bank](36752.md)'s [Worldwide Governance Indicators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worldwide_Governance_Indicators) has also declined, from 33.42/100 in 2018 to 29.75/100 in 2024.[^cite_note-180]

## Stock market

Iran's stock market, the [Tehran Stock Exchange (TSE)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Stock_Exchange), has experienced [boom-and-bust cycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_cycle) in recent years, often moving opposite to the real economy.[^cite_note-181] During recessions and currency devaluation, many Iranians invested in stocks as a hedge against inflation, fueling speculative rallies.[^cite_note-182] In 2019–2020, amid U.S. "[maximum pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_pressure_campaign)" sanctions and a recession, the TSE index surged by over 500% within a year.[^cite_note-183] By late 2020 and into 2021, the bubble burst – the market crashed over 30% from its peak by early 2021, wiping out many small investors' savings. Allegations emerged that government insiders had benefited from selling at highs, causing public mistrust.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185]

Structural issues, such as lack of market depth, high inflation, and political risks, continue to make Iran's stock market volatile. Following the war, analysts argued that without genuine government support (e.g. large capital injections like those used to stabilize the currency), the market downturn could deepen, as the war's aftermath and ongoing sanctions deter both local and foreign investors.[^cite_note-\:4-186]

## Ownership

Further information: [Privatization in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatization_in_Iran) and [Organization for Collection and Sale of State-owned Properties of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_for_Collection_and_Sale_of_State-owned_Properties_of_Iran)

[Social class in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class_in_Iran)

[^cite_note-LOC5-43]

[^cite_note-187]

[^cite_note-188]

[^cite_note-189]

1. Upper class (4.30%)

2. Middle-class (32.0%)

3. Working class (15.0%)

4. [Lower class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underclass)/[relative poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relative_poverty) (42.0%)

5. [Lower class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underclass)/[absolute poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_poverty) (6.70%)

Following the conclusion of [hostilities with Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War), the Government declared its intention to privatize most industries and to liberalize and decentralize the economy.[^cite_note-190] Sale of state-owned companies proceeded slowly, mainly due to opposition by a nationalist majority in the parliament. In 2006, most industries, some 70% of the economy, remained state-owned.[^cite_note-CIA-32] The majority of heavy industries including steel, petrochemicals, copper, automobiles, and machine tools remained in the public sector, with most light industry privately owned.[^cite_note-CIA-32]

Since Article 44 has never been strictly enforced, the private sector has played a much larger role than that outlined in the constitution.[^cite_note-191] In recent years, the role of this sector has increased. A 2004 constitutional amendment allows 80% of state assets to be privatized. Forty percent of such sales are to be conducted through the "[Justice Shares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatization_in_Iran#%22Justice_shares%22_plan)" scheme and the rest through the [Tehran Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Stock_Exchange). The government would retain the remaining 20%.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]

In 2005 government assets were estimated at \$120 billion. Some \$63 billion of such assets were privatized from 2005 to 2010, reducing the government's direct share of GDP from 80% to 40%. Many companies in Iran remain uncompetitive because of mismanagement over the years, thus making privatization less attractive for potential investors.[^cite_note-194] According to then-President [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](7907317.md), 60% of [Iran's wealth is controlled by just 300 people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class_in_Iran#Upper_clas).[^cite_note-195]

### Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps

Main article: [Economic activities of the Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md#Economic_activity)

The [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md) (IRGC) are thought to control about [one third of Iran's economy](1285472.md#Economic_activity) through subsidiaries and trusts.[^cite_note-RAND1-196][^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198] 2007 estimates by the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)* suggest the IRGC has ties to over one hundred companies and annual revenue in excess of \$12 billion, particularly in construction.[^cite_note-199] The [Ministry of Petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Petroleum_of_Iran) awarded the IRGC billions of dollars in [no-bid contracts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-bid_contract) as well as major infrastructure projects.[^cite_note-200]

Tasked with border control, the IRGC maintains a [monopoly on smuggling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smuggling_in_Iran), costing [Iranian companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Iranian_companies) billions of dollars each year.[^cite_note-RAND1-196] Smuggling is encouraged in part by the [generous subsidization of domestic goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_targeted_subsidy_plan#Objectives), including [fuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Gasoline_Rationing_Plan_in_Iran#Fuel_smuggling). The IRGC also runs the [telecommunication company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunication_Company_of_Iran), laser eye-surgery clinics, makes cars, builds bridges and roads and develops oil and gas fields.[^cite_note-NYT20090721-201]

### Religious foundations

Main articles: [Bonyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonyad) and [Setad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Setad)

[Welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_\(financial_aid\)) programs for the needy are managed by more than 30 public agencies alongside semi-state organizations known as *[bonyads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonyad)*, together with several private non-governmental organizations. *Bonyads* are a consortium of over 120 tax-exempt organizations that receive subsidies and religious donations. They answer directly to the [Supreme Leader of Iran](36814344.md) and control over 20% of GDP.[^cite_note-RAND1-196][^cite_note-202] Operating everything from vast soybean and cotton farms to hotels, soft drink, automobile manufacturing, and shipping lines, they are seen as overstaffed, corrupt and generally unprofitable.[^cite_note-Abbas-203]

*Bonyad* companies compete with Iran's unprotected private sector, whose firms complain of the difficulty of competing with the subsidized *bonyads*.[^cite_note-Abbas-203] *Bonyads* are not subject to audit or [Iran's accounting laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Iran#Accounting_standards).[^cite_note-204] *[Setad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Setad)* is a multi-sector business organization, with holdings of 37 companies, and an estimated value of \$95 billion. It is under the control of the Supreme Leader, [Mojtaba Khamenei](23324096.md), and created from [thousands of properties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction_industry_of_Iran#History) confiscated from [Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_peoples).[^cite_note-Setad-205]

## Labor force
