&#x20; Miscellaneous

&#x20; Stone and glass

&#x20; Wood products

&#x20; Animal and vegetable bi-products

&#x20; Animal hides

&#x20; Arts and antiques

&#x20; Weapons

Iran is a founding member of the [OPEC](166346.md) cartel[^cite_note-g417-366] and the [Organization of Gas Exporting Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_Exporting_Countries_Forum).[^cite_note-367] [Petroleum](28205267.md) constitutes 56% of Iran's exports with a value of \$60.2 billion in 2018.[^cite_note-OPEC_2019-19] For the first time, the value of Iran's non-oil exports is expected to reach the value of imports at \$43 billion in 2011.[^cite_note-368] [Pistachios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pistachio), liquefied propane, methanol (methyl alcohol), [hand-woven carpets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_carpet) and [automobiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automobiles) are the major non-oil exports.[^cite_note-369] [Copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Iran#Copper), [cement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Iran#Cement), [leather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_leather_industry), [textiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_of_Iran#Textiles_and_apparel_industry), [fruits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fruit_in_Iran), [saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron) and [caviar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caviar) are also export items of Iran.

Technical and engineering service exports in FY 2007 were \$2.7 billion of which 40% of technical services went to Central Asia and the Caucasus, 30% (\$350 million) to Iraq, and close to 20% (\$205 million) to Africa.[^cite_note-370]

Iranian firms have developed energy, pipelines, irrigation, dams and power generation in different countries. The country has made non-oil exports a priority[^cite_note-AFP-107] by expanding its broad industrial base, educated and motivated workforce and favorable location, which gives it proximity to an estimated market of some 300 million people in [Caspian](19653787.md), Persian Gulf and some [ECO countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Cooperation_Organization) further east.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-ECO-20120120-372]

Total import volume rose by 189% from \$13.7 billion in 2000 to \$39.7 billion in 2005 and \$55.189 billion in 2009.[^cite_note-373] Iran's major commercial partners are [China](29416684.md), [France](4677443.md), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany%E2%80%93Iran_relations), [India](4380684.md), [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran-Italy_relations), [Japan](12653536.md), [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran-Russia_relations), and [South Korea](31095504.md). From 1950 until 1978, the [United States](310477.md) was Iran's foremost economic and military partner, playing a major role in infrastructure and industry modernization.[^cite_note-documentary-44][^cite_note-documentary2-45] It is reported that around 80% of machinery and equipment in Iran is of German origin.[^cite_note-374] In March 2018, Iran had banned Dollar in trade.[^cite_note-375] In July 2018, France, Germany and the UK agreed to continue trade with Iran without using Dollar as a medium of exchange.[^cite_note-376]

Since the mid-1990s, Iran has increased its economic cooperation with other [developing countries](78449.md) in "[South–South integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South%E2%80%93South_cooperation)" including [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_League%E2%80%93Iran_relations#Syria), India, [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Iran_trade_relations), [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran-South_Africa_relations), [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba%E2%80%93Iran_relations), and [Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Venezuela_relations). Iran's trade with India passed \$13 billion in 2007, an 80% increase within a year. Iran is expanding its trade ties with [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran-Turkey_relations) and [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran-Pakistan_relations) and shares with its partners the common objective to create a common market in West and Central Asia through [ECO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Cooperation_Organization).

Since 2003, [Iran](14653.md) has increased investment in neighboring countries such as Iraq and Afghanistan. In [Dubai](211583.md), [UAE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_League%E2%80%93Iran_relations#United_Arab_Emirates), it is estimated that [Iranian expatriates](9573713.md) handle over 20% of its domestic economy and account for an equal proportion of its population.[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] Migrant Iranian workers abroad remitted less than \$2 billion home in 2006.[^cite_note-379] Between 2005 and 2009, trade between Dubai and Iran tripled to \$12 billion; money invested in the local real estate market and import-export businesses, collectively known as the *[Bazaar](556904.md)*, and geared towards providing Iran and other countries with required consumer goods.[^cite_note-380] It is estimated that [one third of Iran's imported goods and exports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smuggling_in_Iran) are delivered through the [black market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_market), underground economy, and illegal jetties, thus [damaging the economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smuggling_in_Iran#Damage_to_the_economy).[^cite_note-RAND1-196]

### Current accounts

Iran had an estimated \$110 billion in [foreign reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_reserves) in 2011[^cite_note-F10-159] and balances its [external payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_payment) by pricing oil at approximately \$75 per barrel.[^cite_note-\:2-160] As of 2013, only \$30 to \$50 billion of those reserves are accessible because of current sanctions.[^cite_note-ABC-161] [Iranian media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_media) has questioned the reason behind Iran's government non-repatriation of [its foreign reserves](3961876.md#Foreign_reserves) before the imposition of the [latest round of sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1929) and its failure to convert into [gold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_bullion). As a consequence, the [Iranian rial](708656.md) lost more than 40% of its value between December 2011 and April 2012.[^cite_note-FT4-140]

The current account was expected to reach a surplus of 2.1% of GDP in FY 2012, and the net fiscal balance (after payments to Iran's [National Development Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Development_Fund)) will register a surplus of 0.3% of GDP.[^cite_note-EIU5-162] In 2013 the [external debts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/External_debt) stood at \$7.2 billion down from \$17.3 billion in 2012.[^cite_note-\:14-163] Overall [fiscal deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_deficit) is expected to deteriorate to 2.7% of GDP in FY 2016 from 1.7% in 2015.[^cite_note-IMF111-164]

[Money in circulation](3961876.md#Money_supply) reached \$700 billion in March 2020 (based on the 2017 [pre-devaluation exchange rate](708656.md#Value)), thus furthering the decline of the [Iranian rial](708656.md) and rise in inflation.[^cite_note-\:15-165][^cite_note-\:16-166]

### Foreign direct investment

Main article: [Foreign direct investment in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment_in_Iran)

See also: [Assalouyeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assalouyeh), [Tehran International Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_International_Fair), and [Iranian citizens abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_citizens_abroad)



[FDI stock in Iran (1980–2010)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment_in_Iran)

In the 1990s and early 2000s, indirect oilfield development agreements were made with foreign firms, including [buyback contracts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Direct_Investment_in_Iran#Buy-back) in the oil sector whereby the contractor provided project finance in return for an allocated production share. Operation transferred to [National Iranian Oil Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Iranian_Oil_Company) (NIOC) after a set number of years, completing the contract.[^cite_note-381]

Unfavorable or complex operating requirements and [international sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Iran) have hindered [foreign investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Direct_Investment_in_Iran) in the country, despite liberalization of relevant regulations in the early 2000s. Iran absorbed \$24.3 billion of foreign investment between the [Iranian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_calendar) years 1993 and 2007.[^cite_note-382] The [EIU](15303116.md) estimates that Iran's net FDI will rise by 100% between 2010 and 2014.[^cite_note-EIU2-383]

Foreign investors concentrated their activities in the energy, vehicle manufacture, copper mining, construction, utilities, petrochemicals, clothing, food and beverages, telecom and pharmaceuticals sectors. Iran is a member of the World Bank's [Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilateral_Investment_Guarantee_Agency).[^cite_note-384] In 2006, the combined net worth of [Iranian citizens abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_citizens_abroad) was about 1.3 trillion dollars.[^cite_note-385]

According to the head of [the Organization for Investment, Economic and Technical Assistance of Iran (OIETAI)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Economic_Affairs_and_Finance_\(Iran\)#Organization_for_Investment_Economic_and_Technical_Assistance), in 2008 Iran ranked 142 among 181 countries in working conditions. Iran stands at number 96 in terms of business start-ups, 165 in obtaining permits, 147 in employment, 147 in asset registration, 84 in obtaining credit, 164 in legal support for investments, 104 in tax payments, 142 in overseas trade, 56 in contract feasibility and 107 in bankruptcy.[^cite_note-386] Firms from over 50 countries invested in Iran between 1992 and 2008, with Asia and Europe the largest participants as shown below:[^cite_note-387]

| Continent of origin | Leading countries investing in Iran (1992–2008) | Number of projects | Total amount invested |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Asia | India, United Arab Emirates (UAE), Singapore, Indonesia and Oman | 190 | \$11.6 billion |
| Europe | Germany, the Netherlands, Spain, UK, Turkey, Italy and France (20 countries in total) | 253 | \$10.9 billion |
| Americas | Canada, Panama, the US and Jamaica | 7 | \$1.4 billion |
| Africa | Mauritius, Liberia and South Africa | —N/a | \$8 billion |
| Australia | Australia | 1 | \$682 million |

The economic impact of a partial [lifting of sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action) extends beyond the energy sector; *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported that "consumer-oriented companies, in particular, could find opportunity in this country with 81 million consumers, many of whom are young and prefer Western products".[^cite_note-Krauss-388] The consumer-goods market is expected to grow by \$100 billion by 2020.[^cite_note-389]

In 2015, Iran was considered "a strong [emerging market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_market) play" by investment and trading firms.[^cite_note-Krausws-390] Opening Iran's market place to [foreign investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment_in_Iran) could also be a boon to competitive multinational firms operating in a variety of manufacturing and service sectors, worth \$600 billion to \$800 billion in new investment opportunities over the next decade.[^cite_note-WSJ3-391][^cite_note-NFTC-392][^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394]

### Foreign currency reserves

In the 2000s, during high oil prices, Iran's central bank built up reserves well above \$100 billion.[^cite_note-\:02-91] However, sanctions after 2012 cut Iran's access to much of its money (funds accumulated from oil sales in countries like China, India, South Korea were locked or could only be used for limited purchases).[^cite_note-395] In 2018, when the U.S. reimposed banking sanctions, Iran's usable reserves shrank dramatically – at one point in 2020, the IMF estimated Iran had as little as \$4 billion in accessible reserves (excluding gold and illiquid assets).[^cite_note-396] After 2021, higher oil exports to China allowed some rebuilding of reserves. By late 2024, Iran's total international reserves (including gold and deposits abroad) were estimated at \$26 billion, rising to a projected \$34 billion in 2025 as per IMF data – though not all of that is readily deployable.[^cite_note-397]

Lack of reserve access has contributed to Iran's currency volatility; the central bank has limited ability to intervene in the open market with dollars or euros.[^cite_note-398] In late 2023, a deal with the U.S. allowed Iran to repatriate about \$6 billion of its funds from South Korea (in exchange for a prisoner release), but those funds were designated for humanitarian trade only.[^cite_note-399] Iran's oil export revenue (mostly in yuan from China) is partly kept offshore to finance imports from those countries.[^cite_note-400]

Facing this, the Central Bank of Iran has tried tactics like currency swap agreements with allies, efforts to ditch the U.S. dollar in trade (using barter or currencies like yuan/rouble), and even reportedly buying cryptocurrencies as alternative reserve assets.[^cite_note-401] In 2022, the bank also raised interest rates and banned private exchange trading for a period to slow capital flight.[^cite_note-\:12-402] Yet capital flight persisted – an estimated \$14 billion left Iran in 2024 and \$20 billion in 2023 as people sought safer assets abroad, reflecting low confidence in the rial.[^cite_note-\:12-402]

### International sanctions

Main articles: [International sanctions against Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Iran), [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action), and [Maximum pressure campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_pressure_campaign)

After the [Iranian Revolution](347268.md) in 1979, the United States ended its [economic and diplomatic ties with Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.-Iran_relations), banned Iranian oil imports and froze approximately \$11 billion of its assets.[^cite_note-403] In 1996, the [U.S. Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Government) passed the [Iran and Libya Sanctions Act](20905961.md) (ILSA) which prohibits U.S. (and non-U.S.) companies from investing and trading with Iran in amounts of more than \$20 million annually.[^cite_note-404] Since 2000 exceptions to this restriction have been made for items including [pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Iran#Pharmaceuticals) and [medical equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Iran#Medical_equipment).[^cite_note-405]

[Iran's nuclear program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran's_nuclear_program) has been the subject of contention with the West since 2006 over suspicions of its intentions. The [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) imposed sanctions against select companies linked to the nuclear program, thus furthering the country's [economic isolation](64709.md).[^cite_note-WSJ2-61] Sanctions notably bar nuclear, missile and many military exports to Iran and target investments in [oil, gas and petrochemicals](28205267.md), exports of refined petroleum products, as well as the [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md), [banks, insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_and_insurance_in_Iran), [financial transactions](3961876.md#Payment_systems) and [shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_Shipping_Lines).[^cite_note-406]

In 2012, the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_sanctions_against_Iran) tightened its own sanctions by joining the [three-decade-old US oil embargo against Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._sanctions_against_Iran).[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-Embargo_WSJ-408] In 2015, Iran and the [P5+1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P5+1) reached a [deal on the nuclear program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action) that will remove the main sanctions by early 2016.[^cite_note-409] Even though Iran can trade in its own currency some problems subsist mainly due to the fact that it cannot transact in [US dollars](18717338.md) freely.[^cite_note-410] Given its large reserves of oil and gas, the [Iranian rial](708656.md) could become a [world reserve currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_reserve_currency) if parity is established with [oil and gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GECF).[^cite_note-411]

In 2018, the [United States government](195149.md) unilaterally withdrew from the [JCPOA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JCPOA) agreement and re-imposed its sanctions on [Iran's oil sales](28205267.md), [petrochemicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Petrochemical_Company), [shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_Shipping_Lines), [metals trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Iran) and [banking transactions](3961876.md).[^cite_note-412]

#### Effects

See also: [Iranian resistance economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resistive_economy#Iran), [Humanitarian impacts of U.S. sanctions against Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanitarian_impacts_of_U.S._sanctions_against_Iran), and [Iranian shadow fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_shadow_fleet)



The [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action) allows Iran [to purchase new planes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airlines_of_Iran).

According to U.S. Undersecretary of State [William J. Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_J._Burns_\(diplomat\)), Iran may be losing as much as \$60 billion annually in energy investment.[^cite_note-Bloomberg_01.12.2010-413] Sanctions are making imports 24% more costly on average.[^cite_note-414] In addition, [the latest round of sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Authorization_Act_for_Fiscal_Year_2012#Sanctions_targeting_the_Iranian_Central_Bank) could cost Iran annually \$50 billion in lost [oil revenues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Petroleum_\(Iran\)#Revenues_from_crude_oil).[^cite_note-415] Iran is increasingly using [barter trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barter_trade) because its access to the international dollar payment system has been denied. According to Iranian officials, large-scale withdrawal by international companies represents an "opportunity" for [domestic companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Iranian_companies) to replace them.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417]

The IEA estimated that Iranian exports fell to a record of 860,000 bpd in September 2012 from 2.2 million bpd at the end of 2011. This fall led to a drop in revenues and clashes on the streets of Tehran when the local currency, the [rial](708656.md), collapsed. September 2012 output was Iran's lowest since 1988.[^cite_note-oil_exports-418]

The economic impacts of sanctions have been severe. Based on research, the sanctions resulted in welfare losses across all income groups in Iran, with wealthier groups suffering greater losses compared to poorer groups.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420] Additionally, income concentration and share within top income groups declined post-sanctions.[^cite_note-421]

According to the U.S. Iran could reduce the world [price of crude petroleum](5137675.md) by 10%, saving the United States annually \$76 billion (at the proximate 2008 world oil price of \$100/bbl).[^cite_note-NFTC-392]

According to [NIAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Iranian_American_Council), sanctions cost the United States over \$175 billion in lost trade and 279,000 lost job opportunities.[^cite_note-NIAC-422] Between 2010 and 2012, sanctions cost the [E.U. states](9317.md) more than twice as much as the United States in terms of lost trade revenue. Germany was hit the hardest, losing between \$23.1 and \$73.0 billion between 2010 and 2012, with Italy and France following at \$13.6–\$42.8 billion and \$10.9–\$34.2 billion respectively.[^cite_note-NIAC-422]

GDP growth turned negative in 2013 (−5%). The unofficial unemployment rate was 20% by mid-2012. [Oil exports](28205267.md) dropped to 1.4 million bpd in 2014 from 2.5 million bpd in 2011. By 2013, Iran had \$80 billion in [foreign exchange reserves frozen overseas](3961876.md#Foreign_reserves). [Automobile production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_cars) declined 40% between 2011 and 2013.[^cite_note-423] According to the U.S. government in 2015, Iran's economy has reached a point where it is "fundamentally incapable of recovery" without a nuclear accommodation with the West.[^cite_note-424]

> The tentative rapprochement between [Iran and the US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States-Iran_relations), which began in the second half of 2013, has the potential to become a world-changing development, and unleash tremendous [geopolitical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_world_order_\(politics\)#Recent_political_usage) and [economic opportunities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_opportunity), if it is sustained \[…] if Iran and the US were to achieve a diplomatic breakthrough, geopolitical tensions in the Middle East could decline sharply, and Iran could come to be perceived as a promising [emerging market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_market) in its own right.[^cite_note-425]

In January 2019, President [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Rouhani) blamed the US for Iran's declining economy. Following the US pullout from an international nuclear deal with Iran and re-imposed sanctions, Iran faced the toughest economic situation in 40 years.[^cite_note-426] According to [Majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis), this has caused damages estimated between 150 and 200 billion dollars to the Iranian economy.[^cite_note-427]

In August 2024, an Iranian group called IRLeaks attacked Iranian banks. [Politico](9172097.md) described the attack as the "worst cyberattack" in Iranian history.[^cite_note-\:12-402][^cite_note-428] According to Politico, the Iranian government was forced to pay millions of dollars to IRLeaks in ransom.[^cite_note-\:12-402] Politico reported that 20 out of about 29 Iranian credit institutions fell in the attack.[^cite_note-Asharq_Al_Aswat-429][^cite_note-\:12-402] The Iranian supreme leader blamed Israel and the United States without mentioning the cyberattack on the banking system. Politico remarked it was plausible Israel or the US were involved because of tensions with Israel as well as the Americans having accused Iran of intervening in the American 2024 elections.[^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431] Politico also remarked that the group was made of freelance hackers and the attack was likely carried out for monetary gain.[^cite_note-\:12-402] TechCentral said the attack was probably carried out by freelance hackers.[^cite_note-\:022-432] An Iranian firm paid the hackers no less than \$3 million to stop the attack and retrieve data.[^cite_note-\:12-402]

## Humanitarian aspects

The U.S. sanctions against Iran are accompanied by negative humanitarian impacts.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-abc-434] According to international law, the United States must assess the impact of its [sanctions](411315.md) on Iranians' rights and address any breaches caused by sanctions. Because of the vast network of US sanctions, banks and firms have withdrawn from humanitarian trade with Iran, leaving Iranians with rare or severe diseases unable to obtain the medicine and treatments needed.[^cite_note-435]In general The devastating effects of sanctions directly and indirectly on health have significantly reduced financial and physical access to drugs and medical equipment in Iran, leading to a substantial decline in public health.[^cite_note-436]

According to [Alena Douhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alena_Douhan), the U.N. special rapporteur on unilateral coercive measures, the groups including "\[people with] severe diseases, disabled people, Afghan refugees, women-led households and children" are adversely affected by the measures. She added that "sanctions have been substantially exacerbating the humanitarian situation in Iran."[^cite_note-abc-434]

## Nuclear program spending

The Iranian regime spent more than US\$9 billion on [Iran's nuclear program](721807.md) over the last three decades.[^cite_note-\:13-89] The efforts to develop the nuclear program were accompanied by threats to [destroy Israel](3022965.md),[^cite_note-437] which resulted in heavy [sanctions that were imposed on Iran](25699056.md) and consequently, heavy indirect cost. The indirect cost of the nuclear program are estimated to be US\$3 trillion.[^cite_note-\:13-89] Iran lost more than US\$450 billion in oil revenues,[^cite_note-438] more than US\$100 billion in lost of foreign investment,[^cite_note-439] and the Iranian Rial has been devaluated in more than 95%.[^cite_note-440]

[Much of the investment was evaporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_Twelve-Day_War) as a result of [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War), which resulted in a destruction of large parts of Iran's nuclear program, including the destruction of facilities like the nuclear facilities in [Natanz](43648558.md) and [Fordow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fordow_Uranium_Enrichment_Plant) as well as assassination of nuclear scientists.[^cite_note-441][^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444]
